People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess I’m here because I haven’t been handling things too well since my daughter left for basic training. I mean, I know kids grow up and I should be proud, and I am, but… the house feels dead when I come home now.

I’ve been trying to keep it together, but I’m crying in my truck before shifts sometimes, and at work I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years. I’ve snapped at some of the younger guys too, which isn’t really me. And I’ve pretty much stopped doing the things I usually like, like going out on the water or working on my sculpture. I just… don’t see the point right now. I’m sorry, I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you need to hear.

I mean, I know kids grow up and I should be proud, and I am, but… the house feels dead when I come home now.\n\nI’ve been trying to keep it together, but I’m crying in my truck before shifts sometimes, and at work I’m missing steps on welding jobs I’ve done for years. I’ve snapped at some of the younger guys too, which isn’t really me. And I’ve pretty much stopped doing the things I usually like, like going out on the water or working on my sculpture. I just… don’t see the point right now. Since she left for basic training, that’s when it really started. I kept thinking it would settle down after a few days, but… yeah, it’s been pretty much since then.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you still enjoy or find pleasure in?\n\nPatient: Not really… I mean, I’m trying not to make it sound worse than it is, but the things I usually like just aren’t doing much for me right now. I’m just… on edge a lot more than I want to admit.\n\nDoctor: Are there any days where you feel a little better and want to do something you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes. I mean… there are moments where I think maybe I should go out on the water or work on my sculpture again, because those used to help me. But then I get home and the house just feels so empty, and I kind of lose the point of it.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic. It’s just since my daughter left, I haven’t really been doing those things. Mostly I’m just trying to get through work without messing up or getting short with people. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… yeah, I think so. I keep wanting to say it’s not that bad, because I’m still going to work and doing what I’m supposed to do, mostly. But since my daughter left, it’s like I come home and the house just feels dead, and then I kind of fall apart.\n\nI’ve been crying in my truck before shifts sometimes, and I’m not really doing the things I used to enjoy, like going out on the water or working on my sculpture. I just… don’t see the point lately. I’m sorry, that sounds awful. But yeah, I guess I do feel sad a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Are there any activities that you've completely stopped doing since your daughter left?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve pretty much stopped going out on the water. I used to do that to clear my head, and I haven’t really been doing it at all since she left.\n\nAnd my sculpture stuff too. I keep telling myself I’ll get back to it, but then I come home and the house feels so empty, and I just… don’t see the point. I know that probably sounds like I’m letting it get to me too much, but that’s what’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything in particular since your daughter left for basic training?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… not guilty about one specific thing, I guess. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I still get through some things because I have to, but it feels like there’s no real enjoyment in it. Since my daughter left for basic, I come home and it’s like the house is just… dead, and I don’t really look forward to anything.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I’m sorry, I know I already kind of said that, but yes. Since my daughter left for basic training, it’s like I come home to this dead house and I just… I can’t get myself to care about the things I used to.

I’ve stopped going out on the water, and I haven’t been working on my sculpture. Those were things I really loved, and now I just don’t see the point. I don’t mean that to sound dramatic, but that’s the honest answer.
